“Shut up, Sokka,” Zuko’s tone had a certain warmth in it too. “Have you ever gone over the side into open water before?” he asked, keeping his voice low.

Sokka shrugged. “I’ve fallen in a few lakes, but mostly you avoid going into the sea at polar temperatures.”

Zuko shook his head. “The water isn’t freezing where we are, but it’s not warm either, and it’s night. When you go in, it slams the air from your lungs and some people inhale the water.” He stepped closer to the door, listening carefully for any sound coming from the other side. He was still counting, just using his fingers to mark the seconds rather than his voice. “Don’t do that, you’ll die,” he added. Presumably in case Sokka was unfamiliar with what happened when you tried to breathe water.

Sokka moved into position behind him. They were really going to do this, all or nothing. “Thanks for the advice, I’ll try not to suck the ocean into my lungs.” If they got caught, he suspected they both planned to go out fighting rather than return to the cell. He shuddered to think of the punishment for an escape attempt, he was pretty sure Zhao would kill Zuko outright rather than risk him getting off his ship. Maybe not right away, but pretty quick and very painful.

“I mean it, Sokka, when you hit the water, get to the surface and float. Don’t do anything else, surface and relax, okay?”

“Got it, I’ll try.” He hadn’t actually thought much about the ocean itself. Getting off the boat had been an all-consuming need and he hadn’t considered much after that. He supposed being with someone who had lived at sea for some time could prove useful. Also, he suspected Zuko might have fallen in (or been pushed) a lot over the years, the way his luck seemed to run.

“Ready?” Zuko asked. His voice was thrumming with all the tension Sokka could feel in himself. “I remember the way. It should be clear of guards until we reach the deck, then we will have to play it by ear.”

Oh well, that filled him with dread. ‘Play it by ear’ sounded less than ideal, but they were very low on alternate options. “Right, you lead, I follow.” Sokka swallowed, he felt like he might puke again. “On deck, if someone spots us, what should we do?”

“Take them out if we can, quietly, if it’s possible. Then just get to the water. We want to go over on the right side and head in that direction.”

“Okay. I’m ready.” He was not sure if he was *actually* ready, but he couldn’t stand the waiting.

It was the moment of truth. Zuko reached out a shaking hand and tried the door, the handle turned and it swung open. Taiju had come through for them. Sokka felt dizzy with relief and he drew in a wobbly breath, his knees trembling. His body was full of energy he hadn’t felt for weeks, excitement, fear and determination. They were getting off this boat, they *were*.

Their bare feet were almost soundless over the metal floors, but their breathing sounded as loud as a drum in Sokka’s ears. Zuko led them carefully and unerringly through the ship, occasionally pausing to listen or to check that the way was clear. The light was dim, but bright enough there would be no hiding if anyone spotted them. Their luck was holding, but as they moved closer to the deck, Sokka’s anxiety mounted. He tried just focusing on Zuko ahead of him, but his eyes kept being drawn to the patches of blood on the shirt. The new ones, lower down where he had been sitting on it. So instead he tried to keep his gaze below the flapping tails and followed the flash of Zuko’s pale legs as he moved.

He had to keep reminding himself - get out first, then have a full breakdown about everything that had happened to them over the past month. Just get *out*.

When they reached the final hatch, Zuko turned to him. “Ready? We have to stay out of view of the command tower, that’s even more important than the guards. The best place for us would be directly underneath it, so that’s what we’ll aim for.”

“Got it.” Sokka suddenly felt strangely calm. All the buzzing had faded into focus. He wondered if this was what warriors felt when the battle started. He hadn’t felt this way before the attack at the North Pole, or when he had gone on the ill-fated mission, he had just felt scared and full of doubt, well deserved, as it turned out.

Now his fear felt like it was tucked down under a blanket of determination. They *would* get off the boat. He would get *Zuko* off the boat if it killed him. But hopefully it wouldn’t. “Let’s go,” he whispered. Even his voice sounded calm.

Zuko nodded, his own face was similarly composed under the bruises. He opened the hatch, and then they were out, two quiet shadows blending in with the flickering dark on deck. Sokka was amazed by the feel of the breeze in his short hair, the touch of it on his skin was like the soft caress of a lost loved one. He took a deep breath, smelt the sea and the stink of the smoke the ship billowed out as it moved. It was incredible, a wonder he had despaired of ever experiencing again. He looked up at the stars, the moon was hidden behind a cloud, but he could see her bright light starting to shine though. As much as he wanted to see the moon, by his estimation she would be full, or close to it and that would be bad news for them. The more light, the more likely it was they would be seen.

Zuko tapped him, pointed in the direction they needed to go and started moving, slow and silent, keeping as close to the metal walls as he could. Sokka followed, watching his step and relishing the night air on his face.

Their luck couldn’t last though, and Zuko held up a hand to stop him. In front of them, leaning on the rail, was a single guard. He wasn’t wearing one of those creepy face plates, but he was armoured and there was a sword at his belt. Zuko looked fierce in the dim light, the darkening bruises on his face and the skin of his scar was like war paint in the shadows. He pointed to the guard, then ran a finger along his throat in what was apparently a universal gesture for murder. Sokka nodded and gave an exaggerated shrug, *how*?

Zuko pointed to his fist, then the guard—that was understandable, but also unlikely to take the guy out. Then he pointed to Sokka and mimed shoving something. Okay, knock the guy overboard with them. This was nuts, but then everything Zuko did was complete madness, and sometimes it even worked for him. Sokka would have to trust in whatever demented guardian spirit had the unfortunate and full-time task of keeping his dumb-arse alive. He nodded.

They slunk forward as quickly as they could together in the gloom, before Zuko moved ahead of him, bare feet barely making a whisper of sound. Sokka hoped he had the strength in him for this. As far as he could tell Zuko hadn’t slept properly in days and hadn’t kept any food down, not to mention Sokka had no idea how injured he actually was. He wished he had actually given him a proper examination, instead of chickening out because he couldn’t stand to see any more evidence of what had happened to him. He was not sure if his imagination was worse than the reality or not.

Zuko turned to him again, made a sharp gesture and then he was stalking forward, swift and despite the sorry state of him, somehow silent and graceful, like a rangy, raw-boned cat striking at a mouse. The guard caught the movement a second too late. He turned quickly, only to receive the full force of Zuko’s fist to his unprotected throat. The impact made a horrible crunching noise, and the man gasped, unable to make a shout. A rush of adrenaline caught Sokka’s body in a tide and he lunged forward hitting the man with everything he had—and perhaps with a little too much momentum, the guard went over the side, but he grabbed at Sokka as he fell, catching his shirt and hanging on.

There was a moment where he could feel the shirt ripping and Zuko’s fingers grabbing at him, but he was off balance and the man was too heavy and he was falling. There was a wild few seconds of being upside-down, hearing the rush of the wind and a shout of his name, and then he was in the water.

It slammed into his face like a brick wall, and then there was cold and dark and rushing swirling as he tried to orientate himself, there was a weight dragging him down and he kicked at it frantically, he was drowning again, and he couldn’t work out which way was up. It was just dark, cold sucking water and pain and panic.

His lungs were burning, but he felt a firm grip on his arm, pulling him upwards, and he kicked. The guard’s hold on his shirt released and he shot towards the surface, desperate for air. His head broke water and he gasped for breath, sucking in sea spray and being swirled up, tossed around like flotsam caught in a riptide.

He got his head above the waves again. Zuko still had a hold on his arm, strong fingers squeezing tight, causing pain from his burns to flare over his skin. “Sokka! Just hold….” There were some garbled noises as the water crashed over Zuko’s head, then he popped up again. “We’re in the ship’s wake, just keep your head above the water!”

Zuko changed his grip to grab him around the bicep, holding onto him tightly and keeping them both at the surface. Sokka gasped and shuddered, legs kicking wildly. He couldn’t catch his breath, it was like the panic again. He hadn’t considered what it would feel like, being under the water, that it would send him back to drowning in the chair. It was in his face, stealing his breath, and now he was drowning, *really* drowning.

“Relax and float, Sokka! Relax, don’t fight it!”

Zuko was a solid presence beside him and he fought to listen to him, to trust his words. He forced his body to stop struggling, to relax, and concentrated on just breathing. The water was calming and although the fear remained, the panic was receding along with the turbulent waves from the ship. He could feel Zuko’s strong grip holding his arm tight enough to bruise.

He kept his head back and opened his eyes to the stars. They were free. Even if they drowned, they were *free.*

But given the option, he would prefer to live. It would not be an easy thing to achieve. The chill from the water was soaking into his bones and with the rush of adrenaline leaving his body he could feel how tired he was, still so weak from hunger and stress. His limbs were starting to feel weighed down and they hadn’t even attempted any actual swimming yet.

There was no sign of the guard that had gone over with him. Perhaps his armour had just dragged him straight down. Sokka shuddered at the thought.

“Let’s swim,” Zuko said. “Keep going towards that star.” He pointed in the general direction of a bunch of stars, which while not completely helpful, at least gave him a general direction.

“Okay,” Sokka gasped. His lungs still felt battered from earlier, but he didn’t think he had breathed in any of the water. He kicked, using his arms in a stroke similar to a doggy paddle. Not the most dignified, but he couldn’t manage anything better.

They swam for a short while, but it felt like the warmth was being sucked out of his skin and the water felt thick against his limbs, like he was trying to move through mud.

“Stop and rest,” Zuko ordered. Sokka was grateful to do as he was told. He was even more grateful when Zuko grabbed at him and pulled him close, he was chilled to the bone too, and the contact offered little warmth, but even a bit was good. Although he felt bad that Zuko wasn’t resting as much as he was, still slowly kicking his legs to keep them up. His shirt was billowing around them in the waves too, giving them a little buoyancy. “Is it better if we float for a bit?” Sokka asked. “At least then we can both rest.”

“This is okay for now. Might have to do that soon. Let’s swim, then rest, swim, then rest. We have to keep moving, but it’s pointless if we drown because of exhaustion.”

They swam. It was slow, but Sokka suspected any attempt at speed would not end well. There was no longer any sign of the ship, and the choppy water was dark and rolling. It seemed to stretch into the night on all sides. He trusted Zuko knew where he was going, he certainly seemed confident. But Sokka was now aware of how much of that apparent surety was actually bluster and bullshit, so he was very much having to trust to hope and that the sea was something Zuko understood.

They did some more swimming, some more cuddling and some more floating, rinse and repeat. Although they were making progress, they were seriously flagging, and Sokka was having real doubts that they were going to make it.

They were doing some attempted resting, hanging onto each other like a pair of limpets, when Zuko made an excited noise, right into Sokka’s ear, startling him from what was worryingly close to a doze.

“Wha?” he said eloquently.

“Wait here,” Zuko said, detangling himself from Sokka’s arms and starting to swim, really swim, powerful and purposeful.

“Wait! Where are you going?” Sokka called after him. He didn’t think he could keep up with him, but he also didn’t think he could bear one second of being alone out here. What if he didn’t come back? Where the fuck was he going? Filled with renewed fear, Sokka floated and tried to manage his breathing. Spirits, but he was cold.

“Sokka!” Zuko’s voice floated out of the dark, strong and excited. “Swim towards my voice!”

Oh well, simple enough. Tiredly, Sokka swam.

He spotted Zuko easily once he had traveled a couple of meters. He appeared out of the dark like an apparition and he was hanging on to a sad, broken bit of tree. Sokka had never been so happy to see something in his life. Renewed, he swam the distance, slung his arm over the wizened, sea worn bark and grinned. Zuko smiled back, although it was small and a little wan. They were going to *live*.

They arranged themselves side by side with the tree wedged under their arms, keeping their faces well clear of the spray, and their legs still in the water so they could move in the right direction. “It’s going to take more work to propel us on this, but if we kick together for five, and then rest for five, we should be able to do it,” Zuko said.

“More counting?”

Zuko snorted lightly. “Again, do you have anything better to do?”

“Nope.”

Zuko started his count, they kicked, and the tree started its slow, ponderous forward motion.

By the time Sokka saw the line of trees on the horizon, his soul felt like it was clinging to his exhausted body by its incorporeal fingernails. He had never felt this physically wrecked before. Beside him, Zuko was also audibly gasping for breath, although the sight of the trees and safety seemed to have doubled his determination and they were moving at a better speed than they had been for the past half an hour. The tide was helping too, drawing them in, rather than away from the shore.

They hit the beach with a bump and disengaged from the tree in the shallows. Sokka attempted to stand in the surf, but the weight of his sodden clothes was too much to handle given how tired he was, and he resorted to crawling through the gentle waves lapping at the sand. Then he was out of the water and hauling himself over the blessedly solid ground like a turtle seal over an ice flow. As soon as he was clear of the waves he flopped down on his belly and turned his head to watch Zuko struggle out of the water in much the same manner he had done, he was also attempting to crawl, but had become entangled in the huge water logged shirt and his knees kept getting caught in it, causing him to fall repeatedly forward, his face narrowly missing hitting the ground each time. He kept going through. Very Zuko of him, Sokka thought, fondly.

Sokka pushed his cheek into the wet sand, relishing the feel and the smell of it. He was freezing and should probably move, but he would give himself a few more moments. Sokka watched with vague, floaty amusement as Zuko made a noise of frustration and backed out of the shirt entirely, flinging it up the beach with a wild, somewhat uncoordinated gesture. He collapsed naked onto the sand and rolled himself over to look up at the sky. That seemed like a good idea, so Sokka did the same. Staring up at the fading stars. They were alive. Both alive and free. If he had any energy, he would have jumped and hollered for joy. As it was, he did a little hooray and a victory dance in his head.

“We should get to shelter,” Zuko muttered. He sounded exactly how Sokka felt: Beyond exhausted. He was right though, no good being free if they just froze to death in the surf. And the tide was coming in, he could feel the waves hitting his feet.

Zuko displayed his usual freakish ability to force his body to do things through the power of stubbornness alone and got to his feet. He grabbed his discarded shirt and staggered up the beach towards the trees. He was not traveling in the straightest of lines, like a man who had just rolled out of a tavern after a little too much liquor and he looked like a stiff breeze might knock him over, but it was still more than Sokka could manage.

But he couldn’t stay here. He didn’t bother trying to stand, and instead began to crawl again. The sand felt weird under his hands, weird but good. It had been so long since there had been anything under his fingers other than the dark, cold metal of the ship. He shuddered at the thought of it.

Zuko had reached the trees and was peering through the dark for a spot that might provide shelter from the elements and hide them from anyone searching. Hopefully it would be another hour or two before their absence was noted, but once discovered, Zhao would work out the most likely places they might have come ashore and he would be hunting for them. He might still trap Sokka’s dad without him actually being aboard, but Zuko? If he was discovered to be alive by the Fire Nation, Zhao would be toast. He was going to have to find him and kill him, or he was completely fucked.

A pleasant thought, but one that made their current position very precarious. Zhao wouldn’t just give up. His entire career, possibly his life, was going to depend on catching Zuko. So they were going to have to rest for a short time and then they were going to have to start running.

Only problem was, neither of them were in any sort of state for such a thing. They would need water come morning, and food if they could find it. And Sokka didn’t know where they were, or which way they should head, other than away from the shore.

“Here,” Zuko called.

Tiredly, Sokka hauled himself to his feet with the help from a battered looking tree and swayed there for a moment on shaky legs, before following Zuko’s voice. He was further into the treeline, wringing out his shirt. He had cleared a small area of debris and clearly intended to sleep there. Sokka staggered closer, stripping off his own torn shirt and dropping it. He didn’t have the strength to wring it out, he would deal with it tomorrow. Instead, he collapsed to the ground on his back and blinked up at the canopy.

Zuko made a grumpy noise, and Sokka glanced at him to catch a look at the scrunchy faced pout that usually accompanied that sound, it was strangely reassuring to find the expected expression on Zuko’s pale, bruised face. If he had the energy, it would have made Sokka smile. Zuko continued to indicate his annoyance by increasing his scowl as he snatched up Sokka’s discarded shirt, squeezing it in his hands to get the water out.

“Take your pants off, you’ll freeze,” he demanded.

“Bossy,” Sokka muttered. But he was right, so he attempted to do as instructed, reaching for his makeshift belt. Only problem was, his fingers felt too thick and tired to undo his knots. It was impossible. Instead, he tried to shimmy them off his hips, the sandy scrub digging into his back as he wiggled, but he had done too good a job at securing them. Defeated, he whined unhappily.

He jumped as Zuko dropped down beside him in a messy sprawl, he planted one pointy elbow in Sokka’s gut, and the other dangerously close to his groin as he used both hands to pick at the knots, growling slightly to himself like a disgruntled kitten. With typical levels of ferocity and stubbornness, he loosened the ties before using the elbow poking Sokka in his belly to leaver himself up. Sokka grunted, winded from the force of it, and attempted a scowl, but Zuko wasn’t watching, he had stumbled the last few steps to where he intended to sleep and flopped down awkwardly. Sokka gave up on his glower and kicked his pants off, leaving them in a soggy tangled mess and dragged his own sorry carcass closer. Not too close, but near enough he could reach out and touch him if he needed to. He felt like he might require more hand holding, but while his emotions were still high, his body felt too weak even for that. Instead, he gazed up at the trees swaying gently above them. “That was not fun,” he said.

Zuko grunted in agreement. “I haven’t had to swim like that in a while. Not since the North Pole,” he said. His voice sounded even dryer than usual. Sokka wished they had water, that was going to be urgent come morning.

“You’ve been in jail since the North Pole,” Sokka couldn’t help but point out. “Why were you even swimming there? That sounds cold.” And suicidal.

“That’s how I got into the fortress. I followed some turtle seals under the ice.”

Sokka pushed himself up on his elbows. “You what?”

“I followed…”

“Yeah, I heard you. Do you know how long turtle seals can hold their breath, Zuko?”

“It worked, didn’t it?”

“Forty minutes, Zuko!”

“It worked!”

Sokka let himself fall back to the sand. He had been wrong, the universe didn’t hate Zuko after all; it seemed to have taken on the full-time job of trying to keep his stupid self alive. Good grief, the guy was a moron.

But somehow, he had ended up being *Sokka’s* moron. He dearly hoped this wasn’t something that resulted in either of their untimely deaths.

He lay quiet for a bit, running over the escape in his mind, trying to process all the fear and the wild joy of freedom. It was hard to pin down his emotions; they felt wild, half soggy and drowned and half zipping on the breeze like snowflakes in a winter storm.

“Hey,” he said, when the silence stretched on a little too long. “Do you think that guy died? The one we pushed into the sea?”

“Yeah.”

“Oh.” Sokka thought about it for a while. Had anyone ever died because of him before? He had saved some lives since leaving home, but he hadn’t really thought about killing people. Not until the boat. But even then it had been a fantasy, one he intended to make a reality but he wasn’t certain how he felt about nameless guard guy. Not sorry he was dead, that was for sure, but strange that his actions had led to it, especially as he had drowned. “Have you ever killed anyone before? Like, in a fight or whatever?” he asked. He picked up a little frond from a rugged, grassy shrub and toyed with it, enjoying the feel on his fingers.

“Yeah.”

Sokka pursed his lips, one-word answers were annoying, and Zuko knew full well it irritated him. “You want to elaborate?” he asked.

Zuko snorted lightly. Even that sounded exhausted. “What do you want to know?”

“How old were you the first time?”

“Fourteen.”

Spirits above and below, he was definitely being difficult on purpose, Sokka just knew it. It made him feel a little warm inside, despite the chill of his drying skin. “*And*? Tell me what happened.”

Zuko sighed, very put upon. “We were in port, uncle told me to stay on the ship, but I snuck out...”

“Why?” Sokka asked. Two could play that game, and he prided himself on his ability to be *very* annoying when he wanted to be.

“There was a guy who had a library, but he wouldn’t let us in.”

“Why not?” Sokka was enjoying the scowl he could see forming on Zuko’s face out of the corner of his eye. He was so easy to rile up.

“It was in the Earth Kingdom, we were clearly Fire Nation.”

“Okay, but why were you trying to go to the library? You snuck off your ship to read books? Not that I object to the idea, libraries are amazing.”

“Obviously I wanted to look at the books!”

“Yeah, but *why*?”

“I thought some of his old scrolls might have information on the Avatar,” Zuko said. He sounded a little sullen, he had to know Sokka would always win at this game.

But something didn’t add up. “Wait, you were hunting for the Avatar two years ago?” That didn’t make any sense.

“Yes. Do you want me to tell this or not?” Zuko said, very pissy.

“I do, please go on, but we are coming back to that point.”

“Well, if you want me to tell you, stop interrupting me!”

“Okay, sorry. So, you snuck off the ship to go break into a guy’s library.” Sokka had no idea where this story was going, he was vaguely hoping it didn’t involve Zuko setting fire to an innocent archivist for denying him books. He didn’t think it would, though. Despite his aggressive nature, Zuko didn’t seem inclined toward randomly murdering people, even when he was furiously angry. He would yell, threaten and punch you, sure, but not indiscriminately kill, he hadn’t hurt the people of Sokka’s village and he had spared Zhao’s life in their duel. That was a fucking shame, if anyone deserved to get burnt to death, it was that bastard. He shivered at the rush of anger that shot through him.

“Right,” Zuko continued. His voice was still tired, but it had picked up a bit of vigour. “And as I was going through the town, someone attacked me. I guess he was trying to rob me.”

That didn’t sound quite right. A single teenager wasn’t a worthwhile mark. “Because kids are often excellent targets for thieves,” he said, probing for more detail. Perhaps he had been recognised as Fire Nation? His eyes *were* distinctive. That might cause them problems if they were going to make it through the Earth Kingdom to…wherever they were going to go. It might cause issues if Sokka persuaded him to come find his dad too, but that was going to be a problem for tomorrow.

“I suppose,” Zuko said, like he hadn’t really thought about it; he probably hadn’t, too intent on getting into the library to register anything else. How had he survived this long? Iroh probably. “Anyway,” Zuko continued. “He attacked me and tried to stab me. Well, he did stab me a bit, but I stabbed him back, several times. I panicked, I guess. And he died.”

“He stabbed you a *bit*?” Zuko’s story telling from his own experience was not like when he recited his poems and plays, *at all*. It was weirdly adorable, even though dragging it out of him was painfully slow. “Then what happened?”

“I puked, and then I passed out because I was bleeding to death.”

Sokka looked at the sky and prayed for strength. “Your uncle found you?”

“Actually, the library man found me and sent word to my uncle. He cauterised the wound and saved me.”

“That was nice of library man.”

“Yeah, I guess it was. He still wouldn’t let me look at his books though.” Zuko glowered at the memory.

Sokka pictured the amusing image of a small Zuko, weak and bloody from being stabbed, attempting to crawl out of his deathbed and demanding to be allowed to investigate dusty old scrolls. And then being carried back to his ship by some burly soldier, yelling the whole way. Sokka would bet all the money in their purse that was exactly what had happened.

Zuko rubbed a sandy hand over his eyes. “I had nightmares about that guy’s face for months. The blood, the way it looked when his life just…stopped. It was awful,” he admitted.

Yeah, that tracked for Sokka’s new and improved image of Zuko. Aggressive, angry and violent, sure, but strangely naïve under all that rage. “He attacked you,” Sokka pointed out.

“I know, it wasn’t wrong that I killed him, and I don’t regret it, not really, but it was still…I was responsible for him being dead. I didn’t like it, even though he deserved it.” He was quiet for a moment. “I don’t think I would have felt as bad if someone else had done it. If my uncle had, or a guard. But doing it myself was…shocking. I’ve killed since then, but not like that.”

“How then?”

Zuko shrugged. “People died when I broke the Avatar out of Pohuai Stronghold. We knocked people off the battlements, the Avatar blew soldiers off ladders in full armour. Some probably lived, but I doubt all of them did.”

“Aang. His name is Aang.” Sokka wondered if Aang had thought about it. Did he realise people had died? Sokka didn’t think so. Aang was a kid and sometimes he did mean or selfish things, or rode on a grumpy animal that wasn’t happy about it, but at heart he was gentle, kind and sensitive to other people’s pain. If he knew he had caused death, it would have hurt him deeply and obviously. “Do me a favour, and if in the future you and Aang ever get a chance to reminisce about that particular prison break, please never tell him. Not unless he *has* to know.”

“If you’re going to take life, you should acknowledge it. It’s not something you should hide from. That dishonours the dead as well as yourself.”

“Yeah, I see that point and I agree, and maybe it’s a lesson he should learn. But please, just don’t. If he needs to know, let me tell him, okay?” Don’t tell him blunt to his face with no prep-work, he meant.

“Fine. But I don’t think he could have missed what happened at the North Pole.”

“*I* missed what happened at the North Pole. Is this the battle or when Zhao killed the moon?” The facts surrounding that bit of information continued to be confusing to Sokka, and somewhat mind-boggling. Zuko had been unhelpful about shedding extra light on it.

Zuko nodded. “After the moon thing. I only saw parts of what happened, when Zhao was taking me to his ship. There was a huge glowing…monster thing. It destroyed half the fleet. The men on those boats would not have survived the cold, maybe the benders, but not the sailors.”

Sokka rubbed at his face tiredly, and with some level of disbelief. “You somehow failed to mention the giant, glowing monster. How did you leave *that* out of the story? You complete weirdo.” He sighed “How’d you know it was Aang?”

Zuko sat up so he could shoot Sokka a full force frowny face at being called a weirdo. “What else could it be?”

What indeed? “Are you mad at him about it?” Sokka asked curiously. He could understand if he was, those were his people after all. But it would complicate an already potentially difficult situation. Sokka didn’t want to have to be in a position where he was stuck in the middle of Zuko and Aang and their different viewpoints on the war. He knew where his loyalties lay, and he had no intention of ever compromising them, but he also didn’t want to abandon Zuko, he didn’t want to lose him to his father’s fucked up ideology. He *knew* there was more to him, knew he had the potential to do the right thing. But that was all it was at the moment, potential.

Another tomorrow problem, that one.

“Mad?” Zuko asked. He sounded a little confused and was clearly giving it some thought. He leaned back on his hands, legs stretched out in front of him. “Shocked, horrified, and a little scared, maybe, but not mad. It’s war. When you attack people, it’s their right to defend themselves. If we had a big glowing fish monster, we would probably use it too.”

“*Fish* monster? Wasn’t the moon a fish too? Are you making this up?” This was still not something Sokka could get his head around. Fish monster? Was he saying Aang had turned into a *fish*? The moon was a fish, *Aang* was a fish, why *fish*? If it was anyone other than Zuko, in any situation other than this one, he really would think he was talking out of his arse.

Zuko shrugged awkwardly. “It had a fishy face, but it didn’t move like a fish. I don’t know. What else could it be but the Avatar?” he said again.

Sokka didn’t have an answer for that. “Speaking of the Avatar...” And leaving glowy fish monsters behind, hopefully. “How come you were hunting him two years ago? He was still in an iceberg then.”

“He was in an iceberg?”

“Er, yeah...Katara cracked it open like an egg and the Avatar fell out.” Okay now it sounded like *he* was making shit up.

“Huh.” Zuko looked pensive, but he also seemed to accept it.

“You didn’t answer my question.”

The little sideways glance he shot Sokka indicated that had been on purpose. “My father…” Zuko began, with obvious reluctance. “The terms of my banishment stated that if I found the Avatar and brought him to the Fire Nation, I could come home. And my father would restore my honour.” Oh. That made everything make sense, Zuko’s obsession, his freakish level of tenacity and single-mindedness when it came to hunting Aang. The Avatar was his ticket home. It was almost unbearably sad, and the level of delusion and self-deception was almost off the charts. “He had been missing for a hundred years, Zuko.”

“But I found him, didn’t I?” Zuko snapped stubbornly.

Yeah, he had. In fact, he had probably pulled him up from the depths of the ocean by the force of his desperation alone, right into Katara’s path. If Sokka believed in destiny, or the spirits meddling in mortal affairs, he might actually believe that. It was nothing but coincidence, of course, that Zuko had just so happened to be sailing through the South Pole on his mad, fruitless quest when Aang popped back into the world. But it still made Sokka shiver, just a little, and goose bumps formed on his arms. He didn’t want to think about this anymore. He curled up, trying to rub some warmth into his limbs. “Don’t suppose you have enough bending to start a fire?” he asked hopefully.

Zuko pulled his knees up and looked at his sandy toes. “I have nothing. Not a spark. I can’t even warm myself.”

It would probably be a terrible idea anyway, lighting a fire so close to the coast, when that bastard Zhao would be hunting them come first light. He sort of wanted to ask Zuko if they could go back to back, like they had in the jail, for the warmth, but also because he just wanted the comfort of it. But he couldn’t bring himself to mention it. He didn’t know how Zuko would feel about the idea of such intimate contact. So he curled a little tighter and shivered.

Zuko made a noise, part grunt and part growl. “Come here,” he demanded.

Sokka turned to look at him. Zuko was pointing to the spot in front of him, as imperious as if he was sitting on his father’s throne.

“Ask nicely,” Sokka said, with a brief smile.

Zuko rolled his eyes, dark and shadowed in the dim predawn light. Sokka crawled over, not willing to push his luck.

“Lie down,” Zuko instructed.

Sokka did, wryly amused by the orders. He would let it fly for now, but tomorrow, they would have words about any further bossiness. Zuko shoved at him, arranging his limbs to his satisfaction, so Sokka was lying on his side, facing away from him. Then he tucked a lovely warm arm around Sokka’s middle and pulled him close until he was wrapped around Sokka’s back. Far, far more intimate than Sokka’s vague idea, but it was just what he wanted. Zuko pushed his face into Sokka’s neck, just below the short hair at the base of his skull.

“Go to sleep, we have to get moving as soon as it's light.” His voice was muffled and reverberated against Sokka’s skin.

“Okay. Good night.”

Having a naked firebender literally breathing down the back of his neck should have been terrifying, but it was somehow the most comforting thing he could imagine, save for his dad or sister swooping in to rescue them. Zuko’s arm tightened a little around him, and then relaxed.

Sokka draped his own arm over the one slung around his waist and closed his eyes. He listened to the weird sounds of the forest, the sea beating against the beach, and felt the strength of Zuko’s heart beating against his back, as his breath slowed and he slipped into sleep. He thought back to the boat. It felt like a lifetime ago, and yet it was so deeply inside him, like a splinter under his skin. He didn’t think he would ever get it out. He wasn’t the same person he had been when he had first been taken captive. The thought scared him, but he didn’t think that person existed anymore. They had thoroughly beaten it out of him. Now when he closed his eyes to imagine something comforting before sleep, he didn’t see his sister, or his father, or some pretty girl who might take a shine to him.

He saw himself killing the people who had hurt him, hurt *them*. Saw himself killing Ro and Chen and that bastard Zhao. He thought about doing it slowly, about making them beg. He didn’t think he would feel the way Zuko had described when he had killed. He thought he would enjoy it.

No, there was no going back for him, to that person he had been before.

He felt Zuko’s hot, slow breath on the back of his neck, and the warm, secure arm holding him tightly.

There was no going back for a lot of things
